Mosca's inequality (x + y > z): x = 10, y = 5, z = 8 → migration would finish too late under this scenario. z is a planning assumption chosen per risk profile, not a prediction. A cryptographically relevant quantum computer is not publicly known to exist today.

Post-quantum maturity: Level 0 of 4 — no post-quantum cryptography. Next step: Inventory where classical public-key cryptography protects long-lived data and keys, and choose the first migration: usually a hybrid TLS key exchange and wrapped keys.

Scope and boundaries

Mode: Document audit

Supplied inputs

Methodology

The audit was performed by the QSI engine 1.3.0. Each analyzer emits facts (rule, parameters, evidence); finding texts are generated from the rule catalog. Methodology sources are listed under "Sources and normative documents".

Prioritization uses an explainable weighted model (severity, exploitability, exposure, confidentiality/integrity/availability impact weighted by the "Crypto exchange, custodian or wallet" profile, asset criticality, urgency, ease of remediation) with a confidence multiplier. CVSS v3.1 is applied only to technical vulnerabilities with a defined vector. Long-term quantum risks use Mosca's inequality; their severity is capped at Medium and they are never presented as exploitable vulnerabilities.

Finding classes: confirmed technical vulnerability; known vulnerable component (a dependency version named in an advisory; whether the affected code is reachable is assessed separately, and a component confirmed by review becomes a vulnerability); confirmed configuration error; potential cryptographic risk; long-term post-quantum risk; improvement recommendation; insufficient data. Heuristic findings are marked preliminary; analyst decisions are shown next to the findings.

Indirect calls (traits, macros, other dependencies) are not followed.

Recommendation

Upgrade to a fixed version (42.7.12).

Re-run the dependency scan after upgrading.

Mosca's inequality (x + y > z)
x=0.0, y=5.0, z=8.0 → time available under this scenario

Potential impact

For authentication, risk materializes only once a CRQC exists while the key/certificate is still trusted. Certificates are short-lived, so the main concern is the PKI and long-lived roots/intermediates.

Post-quantum assessment

Key establishment (x = how long data must stay confidential; harvest now, decrypt later)Signatures (x = how long signatures/keys must stay trusted)
Data/key lifetime x1010
Migration time y55
Assumed years to CRQC z8 (early)8
x + y − z7 → migration would finish too late under this scenario7 → migration would finish too late under this scenario

Planning assumptions: x for key establishment, x for signatures, y are the defaults of the risk profile “Crypto exchange, custodian or wallet”, not the client's figures; with the client's actual lifetimes the verdict can change.

Post-quantum maturity: Level 0 of 4 — no post-quantum cryptography

  • Classical only — key wrapping and data encryption at rest: RSA (1 file(s))

Next step: Inventory where classical public-key cryptography protects long-lived data and keys, and choose the first migration: usually a hybrid TLS key exchange and wrapped keys.

Levels: 0 none; 1 present, not wired; 2 in use outside the critical paths; 3 hybrid in a critical path; 4 post-quantum by default in the critical paths (root of trust, key wrapping, channel key exchange). Judged from code and manifests.

What the quantum-vulnerable algorithms do

RoleAlgorithms (files)IDFirst step
key wrapping and data encryption at restRSA (1)QSI-2026-17449Wrap keys with ML-KEM or a hybrid KEM (X25519 + ML-KEM-768) and re-wrap the stored keys; data that may already have been copied needs its own assessment
authentication and identity (certificates, tokens, device attestation)RSA (4)QSI-2026-17448Keep credentials short-lived and plan ML-DSA certificates, tokens and keys as the ecosystem supports them
role not determinedRSA (3)QSI-2026-17451Classify each usage, record data lifetimes, and plan migration to ML-DSA (FIPS 204) or SLH-DSA (FIPS 205), in a hybrid/composite form where the ecosystem supports it; introduce an abstraction layer for crypto-agility

Roles are judged from file paths, function names and the code around each match; review confirms them. Root-of-trust keys and wrapped keys come first: they are hard to replace or can be recorded today.

z is a planning assumption chosen per risk profile, not a prediction. A cryptographically relevant quantum computer is not publicly known to exist today.

Quantum algorithms relevant to this inventory

Quantum algorithmEffectInventoried algorithms (rows)What it means for this audit
Shor's algorithm — factoring
Q1: quantum computation offline, classical access to the victim		breaks RSA (10)Breaks RSA at every key length: a longer modulus only raises the cost polynomially. Plan migration to ML-KEM (or a hybrid) for key establishment and ML-DSA / SLH-DSA for signatures; start with keys protecting data that must stay confidential for years (harvest now, decrypt later) and with long-lived signatures.
Grover's algorithm — key search and preimages
Q1: quantum computation offline, classical access to the victim		weakens (larger keys restore the margin) HMAC (4), SHA-2 (13), bcrypt (3)Not a break. 256-bit keys (AES-256, ChaCha20) keep a comfortable margin; AES-128 meets NIST category 1, while some authorities ask for 256-bit keys in quantum-resistant contexts (see the AES-128 entry). Prefer 256-bit keys for long-lived data; 256-bit hashes need no change for preimage resistance.
Brassard–Høyer–Tapp — collision search
Q1: quantum computation offline, classical access to the victim		weakens (larger keys restore the margin) SHA-2 (13)256-bit hashes (SHA-256, SHA3-256) remain adequate for collision resistance; 160-bit hashes are already weak classically (SHA-1 collisions are practical). Use 384/512-bit hashes where NIST categories 4–5 are required.

Effects: breaks — replace the algorithm; weakens — larger keys or outputs restore the margin; included in standard parameters — no action at standard parameter sets; only with superposition queries — not applicable to ordinary deployments.

QSI-2026-17449 · P4 · 90 daysroot-cause fix

Plan migration of RSA to ML-DSA (FIPS 204) or SLH-DSA (FIPS 205), in a hybrid/composite form where the ecosystem supports it (or a hybrid scheme) once supported by your libraries, protocols and counterparties.

Rationale
Removes dependence on a quantum-vulnerable public-key primitive.
Expected outcome
The asset uses a NIST-standardized post-quantum or hybrid algorithm.
Complexity
high
Dependencies
Library/protocol support (e.g. OpenSSL ≥ 3.5, HSM firmware), interoperability with peers.
Side effects
Larger keys/signatures, possible performance and compatibility impact.
Tests before change
Interoperability tests in a staging environment.
Tests after change
Verify the negotiated/used algorithm on the production asset.
Closure criteria
Re-audit shows ML-DSA (FIPS 204) or SLH-DSA (FIPS 205), in a hybrid/composite form where the ecosystem supports it or a hybrid scheme in use.

Sources and normative documents

Reviewed: 2026-10-13

  1. A. Chailloux, M. Naya-Plasencia, A. Schrottenloher — An Efficient Quantum Collision Search Algorithm and Implications on Symmetric Cryptography (ASIACRYPT 2017; IACR ePrint 2017/847) — IACR ePrint. https://eprint.iacr.org/2017/847 (peer-reviewed article: Õ(2^{2n/5}) time, O(n) qubits, Õ(2^{n/5}) classical memory)
  2. C. Gidney — How to factor 2048 bit RSA integers with less than a million noisy qubits (arXiv:2505.15917) — arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15917 (preprint (2025-05-21): < 1 million noisy qubits, < 1 week, 0.1% gate error, 1 µs surface-code cycle)
  3. C. Gidney, M. Ekerå — How to factor 2048 bit RSA integers in 8 hours using 20 million noisy qubits (Quantum 5, 433) — Quantum. https://doi.org/10.22331/q-2021-04-15-433 (peer-reviewed article (2021): 10^-3 gate error, 1 µs cycle, 10 µs reaction time)
  4. C. Zalka — Grover's quantum searching algorithm is optimal (Phys. Rev. A 60, 2746, 1999; arXiv:quant-ph/9711070) — APS. https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevA.60.2746 (peer-reviewed article: optimal for any success probability; parallel search gains no more than splitting the space)
  5. Common Vulnerability Scoring System v3.1: Specification Document — FIRST. https://www.first.org/cvss/v3.1/specification-document (final)
  6. D. J. Bernstein — Cost analysis of hash collisions: Will quantum computers make SHARCS obsolete? (2009) — cr.yp.to. https://cr.yp.to/hash/collisioncost-20090823.pdf (paper dated 2009-08-23)
  7. FIPS 203 — Module-Lattice-Based Key-Encapsulation Mechanism Standard (ML-KEM) — NIST. https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/203/final (final (August 2024))
  8. FIPS 204 — Module-Lattice-Based Digital Signature Standard (ML-DSA) — NIST. https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/204/final (final (August 2024))
  9. FIPS 205 — Stateless Hash-Based Digital Signature Standard (SLH-DSA) — NIST. https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/205/final (final (August 2024))

Tables 2 and 4: ECDSA, EdDSA and RSA signatures, finite-field and elliptic-curve DH and MQV, and RSA key establishment are deprecated after 2030 and disallowed after 2035 at every key size — the draft deprecated only the 112-bit level. A composite (hybrid) key establishment stays FIPS 140-validatable while one input comes from an approved scheme.
